Each picture is a small web page the script writes and headless Chrome draws at its exact size; the look is one stylesheet, `marketing/templates/kit.css`, with the website's colours, corners and Sora. `marketing/tools/make_video.py` makes the videos from five screen recordings kept beside them; `tools/render_poses.py` draws the poses in Blender, with the camera, lights and dulled dials of `design/mascot/scripts/render_clips.py`, whose functions it reads and does not change; `tools/demo_data.py` writes the made-up trips.
- **Every screenshot shows made-up trips.** `marketing/demo/MilO-demo-export.json`: 193 trips over eight weeks, in the app's own export format, brought in with Import on an emulator (Android 16, started for this and shut down; the debug build of the branch, unchanged; the phone not touched). The driver and the addresses are the invented ones of the website's sample report.
- **Checks:** `tools/changes_page.py --check` and `spotlessCheck testDebugUnitTest` pass. No line of the app changed, so nothing was added to the list of changes.

**`[DECISION]` What the kit's pictures say, decided for Shawn**
He asked for pictures and left the words to be chosen. Each sentence on a picture is a claim, so each was held to what the app does, and `marketing/README.md`, "What the kit says, and what it does not", lists them all.
- **The headline is the website's,** "Business mileage that logs itself.", and the three facts under it are "Free · No account · No ads". The eight Google Play headlines, in order: the headline; "It starts when your phone connects to your work vehicle."; "The drive is recorded with GPS."; "Business or Personal, by your work hours."; "Say what each trip was for."; "The month's report for your accountant."; "Your trips stay on your phone."; "And MilO waves hello."
- **Four sentences were taken back after a second look.** "Change any trip with one press" (it is two: the trip, then the button) is "You can change any trip yourself". "Nobody remembers to log every trip" is "A mileage log is easy to forget". The report's "purpose" is "the purpose you gave it", because a trip without a label has none. And the sentence about what leaves the phone now names Android's address lookup, as the website's does.
- **Left out on purpose, and listed in the README so that nobody adds them early:** Google Play's badge or "Get it on Google Play" (MilO is in review), "open source" (the code is public to read, all rights reserved), anything about Android Auto working in a car, ratings and numbers of users, other products' names and logos, a tax office's approval, and dollars as a promise.
- **The look.** Lime and dark take turns, with at most one lime ground in a picture, as on Home. The phone is a flat rounded frame drawn in the stylesheet, with no shadow, because the design has none and no device frame was to be taken from anywhere. The mascot stands on the lime, as on the website.
- **The demo driver is the sample report's:** "Alex Driver", "Example Electric Ltd.", "2023 Pickup", and its invented streets. One made-up person across the website and the kit. The vehicle is "Work truck", the odometer 86,412 km, the rate the app's own 70 cents. The screenshots' day is Thursday 24 September 2026: a month nearly whole (1,192.1 km of Business, $834) and August already reported.
- **The Android Auto picture is a drawing,** after the real one from Shawn's phone, which shows his own figures and is in no file of the kit. It leaves out the other apps' icons along a car screen's lower edge and is to be used with the words "Android Auto screen" and nothing more.
- **More than the 40 to 60 pictures asked for.** The mascot's seven poses in four forms are 28 files, and the logo's forms 22. Still under 30 MB: the plain screenshots and the see-through poses were made smaller with `pngquant`, which cannot be told from the originals at full size, and the opening is kept as a GIF only, not also as an animated WebP.

**`[DECISION]` The video's opening is the app's own, recorded; three later scenes are edited and say so**
- **Recorded, not rebuilt.** The task allowed a rebuilt opening if the emulator stuttered. It did not need it: of four takes of a fresh start, the smoothest has all 144 frames in which the mascot is seen, on average 42.0 thousandths of a second apart, which is the app's 24 a second, and never more than 52. The other three had two to four gaps of up to 81. What is added is a camera: the picture moves in on him as he reaches the middle and out again as he fades.
- **30 frames a second from 24.** Some of his frames last one of the video's and some two. That is not seen at his walking pace, but the video is a little less even than a phone.
- **"A trip starts by itself" is two pieces of one recording, joined,** with half a second cut out between "Connecting…" and the trip (next entry). **"Recorded with GPS" is three minutes played 42 times faster,** and its words on the screen say "Shown here sped up". The Trips and report scenes play one and a half times faster. The README says all of this.
- **The sound is the app's own two** and nothing else: the connect sound where the trip starts, the trip-start sound where the drive is under way. No music, no voice.
- **The end card is lime,** with the mark, the headline, the three facts, milotriplog.top, and the mascot, who waves once.

**`[FINDING]` An emulator can show the pairing and the vehicle's arrival, but never "Connected"**
Two of Android's own test commands gave the screenshots what an emulator has no vehicle for. `cmd companiondevice associate 0 com.shawnkowalchuk.milo <address>` makes the association; with the truck's name and address from the imported file, the next start of MilO found it and Setup read "Truck paired and watched", 10 of 10. `cmd companiondevice simulate-device-event <id> 2` tells MilO the vehicle is connected by Bluetooth: Home showed "Connecting… Found the truck over Bluetooth" for two and a half seconds, a trip was "started by TRUCK", and the connect sound's moment came. Fifteen seconds later MilO discarded that trip, rightly: "discarded as a false start: the truck's connection was never confirmed", because the reading of the Bluetooth link says the truth. So during any recorded trip on an emulator the small tile says "Truck · Not connected" and the widget "Truck not connected", and for half a second after "Connecting…" the large tile does too.
- **What that costs the kit:** `home-recording.png`, Google Play's third screenshot, the second story and two scenes of the video show a trip recorded with the truck "Not connected". True of a trip started with the button; not what a driver in a truck sees. `[DEBT]` below.
- A change to the app that pretends a vehicle is connected was not made: the task was to show the app as it is.

**`[FINDING]` What it took to make pictures and a video on this Mac**
- **Headless Chrome writes its screenshot and then does not go.** `--screenshot` returned the picture within a second or two and the process stayed for minutes, with eight helper processes; the first run of the build hung on it for five minutes. `build.py` now starts Chrome, waits until the file is written and no longer grows, and ends it (`chrome()`). A run of all pictures takes 40 seconds.
- **A headless window narrower than about 500 pixels is not honoured.** Small pictures (the 240-pixel thumbnail) are drawn in a 600-pixel window and cut out of its corner.
- **`screenrecord` writes a frame only when the screen changes.** A recording of a still screen has seconds without one. Cut with ffmpeg's `-ss`, such a recording gave a scene that began a second and a half early, and the trip never came into it. The recordings are now brought to an even 30 frames a second first, and cut by the clock after that (`fps=30,trim=…`).
- **MilO's clock holds against the emulator's date being set** (ADR-006), which is the point of it. To take screenshots on a chosen day and minute, MilO was stopped, its anchor file removed (`no_backup/clock/anchor`) and the date set before it was started again.
- **This Mac's ffmpeg cannot write an animated WebP.** Pillow can; `mascot/animated/wave.webp` is packed with it.
- **The mascot's see-through pictures are large as plain PNG:** 2.4 MB each at 2048 pixels, 0.37 MB after `pngquant`.
- **The emulator's address lookup works,** and gave the made-up drive a real street: "4730 Gateway Boulevard Northwest, Edmonton". It is a commercial street and stays in the pictures; the README says so.

**`[DEBT]` The kit's rough edges**
- **"Truck · Not connected" in the pictures of a recorded trip** (the finding above). Cost to fix: a debug-only stand-in for the vehicle's connection, which is a change to the app and its own decision; or a screenshot from the phone, which would show real places.
- **Nothing checks the kit's words against the app.** A headline can become untrue when the app changes, and no test says so. The README lists every claim; reading that list is a step for each release that touches what a picture shows. Ten minutes.
- **The screenshots are taken by hand.** `build.py` makes the pictures from them, but no script drives the emulator through the 27 screens. An hour to take them again, with the steps in the README.
- **`mascot/animated/wave.webp` is made by two commands, not by a script:** `render_clips.py` at 720 pixels for `Wave`, and a few lines of Pillow. They are written in the README.
- **The website still shows its older screenshots.** The kit's are newer and show made-up trips; putting them on the website is a change to `website/` and was not part of this.

**`[FINDING]` The minified 0.3.1 on the phone: it installs over 0.3.0, the mascot greets, the car screen draws**
The first release build that went through R8 (ADR-008) was built with his key (12.1 MB) and installed over the phone's 0.3.0 on 2026-10-10 at 13:14. What was seen: "Success", the process up again, nothing in the crash log; Shawn: "mascot walks in and waves, email support works"; and in the Desktop Head Unit the car screen with its two rows, its icons and its lime button, with the month's figures of the evening before. The car screen was the part an emulator could not show, because the Car App Library hands its templates to another app. **Still open before 0.3.1 is released:** a real trip with the truck (device check R8-3), which nothing but the truck can show.

**`[FINDING]` A second adb server can hold the phone, and then `adb devices` shows nothing**
The Mac's USB list had the phone and `adb devices` was empty, also after the server was restarted twice. An adb server on port 5038, running since 2026-10-05 (an earlier session started it), had claimed the phone's debugging interface the moment it was plugged in; only one program can hold it. `ANDROID_ADB_SERVER_PORT=5038 adb devices` listed it. The install and the head unit's forward went through that server. To tell the two cases apart: `ioreg -p IOUSB -l | grep ac7c54e` says whether the Mac sees the phone at all, and `pgrep -fl "adb -L"` lists the servers.

**`[CHANGE]` The release build goes through R8**
The Play Console flagged the bundle of 0.3.0: "DEX code optimization is below our threshold. Improve your percentages in the following categories: Obfuscation (1%). Percentages under 25% in any category of your app may impact your visibility and publishing capabilities on Google Play. Fix by Feb 2027", with "No R8 metadata included" and "Total uncompressed DEX size 32.8 MB". Shawn: "we should fix this". R8 is the tool in Android's build that removes unused code, rewrites the rest to be smaller and gives it short names; it had never been switched on. Now it is, for the release build only (ADR-008): three settings in `app/build.gradle.kts` and a new `app/proguard-rules.pro` with three rules, each with its reason. No library, no version and no line of the app's code changed.
- **Sizes, before and after,** built on this Mac from the same code. The APK: 39,925,176 bytes, now 12,115,227 (39.9 MB, now 12.1 MB). The code in it: 32,782,152 bytes in three files, now 5,280,552 in one (32.8 MB, now 5.3 MB). The bundle for Google Play: 15,508,170 bytes, now 10,134,834, of which 3.9 MB is the mapping file Google keeps for itself; what a current phone downloads from Google Play is, added up from the bundle's parts, about 4.3 MB where it was about 13.5 MB. The resources: 1,208 entries, now 981; all 227 removed were the libraries' (Google sign-in buttons, old notification layouts), none of MilO's 767.
- **R8 gave no warning.** A full release build with everything made afresh takes 34 to 40 seconds on this Mac where it took 28.
- **What stayed the same, checked in the built file:** the manifest, line for line; both mascot pictures, both sounds, the font and the list of changes, byte for byte; every class the manifest names; the Android Auto host list with its six entries; 299 classes of the Car App Library under their own names.
- **Run on an emulator** (Android 16, started for this and shut down afterwards, the build signed with the throwaway debug key, the Keychain not read, the phone not touched), in the minified build: the first start, the welcome page and Setup's ten rows. A trip by hand over a simulated 2.1 km with two turns: 2,062 m, "86 fixes stored, 86 used", a fix every 2.0 seconds in the export. A second trip of 3.3 km with the process crashed on purpose in the middle (`am crash`) and force-stopped later: Android brought the trip service back within a second after the crash ("Trip service in the foreground (restarted by Android)"), the force stop was picked up when MilO was opened, and the trip closed at 3,349 m of 3,360 with gaps of 6 and 11 seconds. Trips: the month, a day opened, a trip marked Business, edited on the edit screen, deleted and restored; a missed trip added by hand. Settings: every tile and every "About"; two numbers and the unit changed and still there after a restart; miles and back. Export, then an import of that file after a trip had been deleted and a number changed: a second export was equal to the first in every part but its time. Three broken files, each refused with its reason. The report's PDF (seen in a viewer) and CSV, the hand-over to Gmail, "I sent it". What's new. The Log with its five filters. The greeting, both pictures. The trip's notification. The widget, added from Settings, with its Start and End. Both sounds ("Connect sound: playing the bundled chirp"). MilO ended in the background on the Report screen and brought back: the Report screen again, with Trips under it. Android's backup with its test transport: backed up, removed, installed, restored, both trips back.
- **Against the unminified build,** on the same emulator: the same drive gave 2,069 m and 85 fixes; Setup's and Settings' words were the same to the letter; the widget's drawing was the same pixel for pixel; every kind of Log line the unminified build wrote, the minified one wrote in the same words. **And the minified build was installed over the unminified one,** as it will be on the phone: the trip, its 85 points and the settings were unchanged in the export, the Log had "MilO was updated", and a new trip recorded.
- **Not run: the Android Auto screen.** No emulator shows it. In its place, in a throwaway build that was never committed, the Car App Library's own packer sent a screen like MilO's (two rows, a coloured button with a press, a header) into a bundle and back, equal, under the library's own class and field names. That is the app's half; Android Auto's half is device check R8-4.
- **Not run either:** anything with the truck, Bluetooth or HyperOS; the real signing key; a Gmail draft (the emulator has no account); the phone. Device checks R8-1 to R8-10.
- **Checks:** `spotlessCheck lintDebug testDebugUnitTest assembleDebug` passes (2,585 unit tests), and `tools/changes_page.py --check`. The debug build is as it was.

**`[DECISION]` MilO's own names are kept, and so is its code's shape; an exception keeps its name**
The event log prints MilO's class names and stack traces, and Shawn reads it on his phone. So `-keepnames class com.shawnkowalchuk.milo.** { *; }`: every name of MilO's stays, what MilO does not use is still removed, and R8 does not fold MilO's methods into one another. Four ways were built and measured. "Score" is R8's own figure, written into the bundle: the share of classes, methods and fields it was free to rename, to rewrite, to remove.

| What is kept of MilO | APK | Code | Renaming score | Rewriting score | Methods with a new name |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Every name, and the code's shape (**taken**) | 12.1 MB | 5.28 MB | 57 % | 57 % | 36 % |
| Every name, shape not kept (`allowoptimization`) | 11.6 MB | 4.76 MB | 63 % | 92 % | 39 % |
| Class names only | 11.4 MB | 4.54 MB | 88 % | 87 % | 64 % |
| Nothing | 11.1 MB | 4.21 MB | 92 % | 91 % | 62 % |

- **Decided for Shawn: the first row.** The second saves half a megabyte and folds away 3,768 of MilO's 10,709 methods, so a stack trace would miss a third of MilO's calls; and it lets R8 rearrange the code that records trips, which is the code least proven under R8. With the first row, what R8 took out of MilO's own code is only what nothing uses: previews, and the unused methods the compiler makes for data classes.
- **Decided for Shawn: one more rule,** `-keepnames class * extends java.lang.Throwable`. The first line of a stack trace is the exception's class, and the crash file stores it. About 60 classes of the libraries keep their names for it; R8's score moved by less than a tenth of a per cent.
- **Not decided, his if it comes up:** the third row. It is the step to take if Google's own figure for renaming turns out low. It would leave MilO's class names in a trace and nothing else.

**`[FINDING]` With R8, a stack trace names the class and the method, but no longer the file and the line**
The brief for this work expected MilO's own lines in a trace to keep their file names and line numbers, because the rules keep both (`-keepattributes SourceFile,LineNumberTable`). R8 9.4 does not work that way. In every class, kept or not, it puts the id of the build's mapping file where the file name was, and a number of its own where the line was. Seen in a throwaway build with a failure put in on purpose, in the Log's detail: `at com.shawnkowalchuk.milo.data.trip.TripRepository.setCategoryByHand(r8-map-id-5288aac6…:124)`. The real line was 229.
- **With the mapping file it comes back whole:** R8's own tool, already on the Mac inside the Android build, gave `TripRepository.setCategoryByHand(TripRepository.kt:229)` in under a second, and the libraries' lines their real names (README, "Reading a stack trace from a release"). Without the two attributes there would be nothing to turn back, so they stay.
- **So the mapping file is needed for MilO's own lines too,** not only for the libraries'. A release whose mapping file is lost keeps half-readable traces.
- **The id is useful:** it is the `pg_map_id` at the top of that build's `mapping.txt`, so a trace says which file it needs. Two builds of the same code have the same id: made afresh three times, R8 gave the same code and the same mapping file, byte for byte.
- **No supported switch turns this off.** R8 has an internal one for the file name; it is undocumented, could vanish with any update of the Android build, and would leave a real file name beside a wrong line number, which misleads more than an id does. Left alone.
- **A few lines in a trace show a short name inside one of MilO's classes** (`TripsScreenKt.m`). Those are small helpers R8 makes itself; they are in nobody's source code.
- A trace is no longer than before: the long id is made up for by the libraries' short names (7,349 characters where the turned-back one has 7,896).

**`[DECISION]` Each release keeps its mapping file: inside the bundle, and attached to the GitHub release**
The bundle for Google Play carries `mapping.txt` by itself; it was found inside the built bundle, byte for byte the file beside the APK. That also ends the Console's warning about a missing file for reading crash reports. For GitHub, the release steps now pack it (53 MB becomes 3.8 MB) and attach it to the release as `MilO-<version>-mapping.txt.gz`, beside the APK. It is not committed: it is build output under `app/build/`, which git ignores. The APK and the bundle are built in one command, so one file serves both. 0.1.0 to 0.3.0 were not minified and have none.

**`[FINDING]` By R8's count 57 % of the app can be renamed; counted by methods alone it is 36 %**
Google does not say how the Console arrives at its "Obfuscation" figure. Three counts of this build. R8's own, which it writes into the bundle and which its report describes as the share of "classes/fields/methods R8 was free to rename": 57.2 % (and 56.7 % free to be rewritten, 93.5 % free to be removed). Counted from the built file against the mapping file: 60.2 % of the classes have a new name (3,914 of 6,499), 58.6 % of the fields, 36.2 % of the methods (9,143 of 25,267, constructors not counted, as nothing can rename them). All are above Google's 25 %.
- **Why the methods are low:** MilO's own code is 42 % of the methods that are left after R8 has removed the libraries' unused ones, and its names are kept. And no app can rename a method that answers to Android or to Java (`toString`, `equals`, `onCreate`): even with nothing of MilO kept, only 62 % of the methods get a new name.
- **The unminified 0.3.0, counted the same way,** has about 1 % of names that look renamed (Play services' own short names), which matches the Console's "1%". So the Console can count names by itself; whether it now takes R8's figure or its own count is not known until the first minified bundle is uploaded (device check R8-9).
- The brief for this work set "about 40 %" as the line under which to stop and ask. R8's figure and the classes are above it, the methods alone are under it. Nothing of MilO's was renamed to move a figure.

**`[FINDING]` Two things this work was told to look for are not there**
- **An import of a broken file leaves no stack trace.** It is refused in words: "A file picked for an import was refused, and nothing was changed: The text is not complete JSON: it ends before a bracket is closed", "…Trip number 1 cannot be read: Unexpected JSON token at offset 156: Failed to parse type 'double' for input 'far' at path: $.distanceMetres", "…Trip 1: its status is "DRIVEN", and only a closed trip can be in an export". All three were seen in the minified build. To see a stack trace, a failure had to be put into a throwaway build.
- **The trip's notification has no End button.** It has a title, the distance and a running clock, and a tap opens MilO; the same in the unminified build. End is on Home, on the widget and on the Android Auto screen.

**`[FINDING]` Two traps when driving MilO on an emulator from the command line**
- **A screen that never comes to rest cannot be read as text.** The widget shows a running clock during a trip, so the home screen redraws every second, `uiautomator dump` gives up, and the file of an earlier dump is still there to be read. That looked for a few minutes like a widget that did not follow the trip. A screenshot showed it did. Remove the old dump before each new one.
- **`monkey -p … -c LAUNCHER` on a MilO that was started with `am start -n` puts a second MilO screen on top of the first.** It looked like a back stack that was not restored. Bring the task back with the same `am start -n`.

**`[DEBT]` CI does not build the release build**
CI makes and tests the debug build, which R8 does not touch. An update of a library or of the Android build that makes R8 fail, or that needs a new rule, is therefore found on the day of a release and not in the update's pull request. What a fault of R8's does at run time no CI can see; that is what installing the release on the phone is for. Cost to fix the first half: one more task in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `assembleRelease` (CI has no key, so it comes out unsigned), about 40 seconds a run. Not done here: it changes what every pull request waits for, which is Shawn's to decide.

**`[FIX]` The Support link is in the website's menu and in the README, not only in the footer**
The link went live in every page's footer and in two paragraphs, and Shawn asked an hour later: "do we not have the suppot email link on the website? What about on github?". He had looked where a visitor looks first, the menu at the top, and it was not there; and the README, which is the project's front page on GitHub, still said "there is no support". Now "Support" is the fifth entry of the menu on all four pages, and the README has it in its top line and in the sentence about the project. A link a person has to scroll to the end of a page for is not a support link.

**`[CHANGE]` The website's mascot: his needles keep moving, and he waves for as long as the pointer stays**
Website only; nothing in the app changes. Shawn saw the mascot on the live site ("yes mascot looks great") and asked: "for the website can we change the mascot animation. can we have the hands for his eyes continuely moving when non rolled over. and when you rollover him he continuely waves tell you stop rolling over him". New: `website/mascot/dials.webp` (8 KB) and `waving.webp` (228 KB, in place of `wave.webp`, 498 KB), two spans for the dials in `index.html`, the rules in `styles.css`, `render_dials.py`, and two clips in the model. APP_ENCYCLOPEDIA, Website, step 7.
- **Decided for Shawn, the needles:** an engine ticking over, not a metronome. The RPM needle sits at 900 and is revved three times in 7 seconds, up fast and down slower; the speed needle wanders between 0 and 36 km/h in 11 seconds. Different times, so the pair repeats only after 77 seconds. His body stands still. A breath drawn frame by frame would weigh on every visitor, and one made by stretching the picture could not be judged from still screenshots.
- **Decided for Shawn, the wave:** the arm goes up once, in under half a second, then the hand waves twice a second until the pointer leaves, when he stands again at once. `Wave`'s own beat is two and a half a second, which is 9.6 frames and cannot be looped frame by frame; at two a second a wave is 12 frames.
- **A tap now makes him wave on a touch screen,** until the next tap elsewhere. The first version had shut touch screens out by rule. Nothing was built for it: a phone's browser treats a tap as a pointer that has come and stays. So half of yesterday's `[DEBT]` is closed without a script, and the other half, a wave that plays on after the pointer has left, no longer applies: he waves for as long as it stays.
- **What a visitor fetches,** measured with the browser's cache off: with the page 866,091 bytes where it was 853,912 (12 KB more, for the dials and the longer stylesheet); at the first rollover 227,916 where it was 497,512 (270 KB less, because the strip has 11 frames and not 24).
- **Checked** in headless Chrome at nine widths from 360 to 1440: the mascot's box and the tile are where and as large as before at every one. At rest, eight screenshots a third of a second apart all differ, and every changed pixel lies inside the two dials' squares. With a simulated pointer on him the strip's frames come 0 to 4 once and then 5 to 10 round and round, five times in 2.6 seconds; off him he stands and the needles go on. With "less motion" set nothing moves and neither new picture is fetched. On a simulated phone the needles move, a tap makes him wave, a tap elsewhere stops it. On a simulated slow line he stands, needles moving, until the strip is there, then waves. **Not checked:** a hand on a mouse, Safari, Firefox, a real phone (a tap on an iPhone least of all). Not deployed.
- **The needles never stop** for a visitor who has not asked their system for less motion, and the page has no button of its own to stop them.

**`[DECISION]` The needles are turned by the stylesheet, not drawn frame by frame**
Only the needles move while he stands, and a needle only turns. So `dials.webp` holds each dial's face without a needle and each needle's blade alone, pointing up; the stylesheet lays the face over the standing picture's dial and turns the blade over it. 8 KB, where a strip of the dials frame by frame was reckoned at 150 KB or more, which was the limit set for it; and the needles move as smoothly as the screen shows, where a strip would have stepped.
- **Without the stylesheet, or with "less motion", he is whole:** `standing.webp` still has its own needles. The faces cover them only when `dials.webp` is there, and both faces and blades are in that one file, so there is no moment with two needles or none.
- **The blade is drawn alone** (`render_dials.py`), with its black cap cut out of it as a hole, because the cap lies over the blade. Turned to where the standing picture has its needle, it covers it: the camera looks at the dials about 6 degrees off square, which is under half a pixel at the needle's tip.
- **The wave's frames carry their own needles** and cover the turning ones, as they cover the rest of the standing mascot. So the needles jump when he starts to wave and when he stops. That was not seen in motion, only in screenshots; it cannot be had otherwise without a script.

**`[FINDING]` A picture the stylesheet counts on needs a new name when it changes**
`firebase.json` lets a browser keep the site's pictures for a week and its stylesheet for an hour. The wave's strip went from 24 frames to 11. Under its old name a returning visitor would for a week have had the new rules stepping through the old strip: more than two mascots squeezed into the box at a time. So it is `waving.webp` now and `wave.webp` is gone; a visitor with the old stylesheet, for an hour at most, gets no wave. **For the same reason he stands in the new `standing.webp` exactly where he stood:** the dials' faces are laid over that picture, old or new. The picture's size is measured from the clips drawn, and from the two new clips alone he came out 2 pixels to the left; so `Wave` is still named when the pictures are drawn (README). Compared with the live picture: the same outline, 15 pixels of 437,760 off by more than 12 of 255.

**`[CHANGE]` Two clips more in the model: WaveUp and WaveLoop**
For the website's wave that goes on. `WaveLoop` (0.5 s, loops) is the arm held up and one wave of the hand; `WaveUp` (0.5 s) is the way into it from standing, and ends where the loop begins instead of in the standing pose as the other clips do. Nothing brings the arm down again: on the website he simply stands when the pointer leaves. The wave's pose is now one function that `Wave` and the two new clips share. **The ten old clips are not changed:** the rebuilt file has them the same in every key, bit for bit, `Wave` among them. The `.glb` is 2.28 MB with twelve clips. The app's pictures were not drawn again.

**`[CHANGE]` MilO Trip Log has a Wikidata item, and the website links to it**
Shawn made the item himself on wikidata.org: Q141683130, "MilO Trip Log", described as "mileage tracking app for Android", with the aliases "MilO" and "Milo Trip Log". This session cannot reach wikidata.org. The statements, added by hand in Wikidata's own editor because QuickStatements, which adds them from one paste, needs an "autoconfirmed" account (4 days old and 50 edits): instance of mobile app, operating system Android, official website, source code repository URL, programmed in Kotlin, version 0.3.0 (the GitHub Release of that evening), publication date 2026-10-09 (0.1.0, the first release), official name, short name and language English. They went in without references at first; each is to carry the website or the GitHub repository or release as its reference. **A slip worth knowing:** "official app" is not "instance of". It says which app an organisation publishes; one saying MilO's official app was "mileage" went in by mistake, with "instance of" missing. He swapped them the same evening, and the warning marks went from every statement but two, so the missing "instance of" was their reason: the other statements' rules expect a piece of software. Of the two left, the version's went when its reference was added, and the repository's when it was given the qualifiers "version control system" Git and "web interface software" GitHub. What remains are flags beside three statements, which are Wikidata's suggestions, not errors. **Left off on purpose:** country of origin, because no public page says it; developer, because it would put Shawn's name on the item; the Google Play app ID, because whether the Play listing is public is unclear (ARCHITECTURE says nothing is uploaded yet, this log on 2026-10-09 that 0.2.0 went to open testing). The landing page's `SoftwareApplication` data now has `sameAs` with the item's address and the repository's. Nothing a visitor sees changed, so the page was not drawn again. Website only, and no line in the list of changes: nobody using the app will notice it. A newer GitHub Release is a new version statement on the item, added by hand; nothing here updates it.

**`[FINDING]` The Wikidata item can be deleted until someone else writes about MilO**
Wikidata's notability policy (WD:N, criterion 2) asks for "serious and publicly available references". Every reference on the item is MilO's own, the website and GitHub, so it can be nominated for deletion. Shawn was told so before he made it. What makes it safe is independent coverage: a review or an article about MilO that is not his. When one exists, add it to the item as a reference. Until then nothing here depends on the item: if it is deleted, its address comes out of `sameAs`.

**`[CHANGE]` Email support: a link on the website and a tile in Settings**
Shawn: "what about adding a support link? can we have it send email to me?". Asked where, he chose the website and the app; nothing on Android Auto. **Website:** a Support link in every page's footer, and the three places that said "there is no support" now say that one person answers email when he has time, because "no support" would be untrue beside a support link and "support" alone would promise a desk that does not exist. **App (phone):** a tile "Email support" above Buy me a coffee, which opens the email app on a draft that ends with MilO's version, the Android version and the phone's model. MilO still sends nothing and still has no internet permission: the email app sends, if the person does. The privacy policy says what the draft carries. Not merged until a test email had reached him (device check SP-1): it did, the same day, and **Gmail filed it under Spam.** Mail that a forwarder passes on often is: it arrives from Namecheap's servers under someone else's name. The cure is on his side of the mailbox, a Gmail filter for mail to the support address that never sends it to Spam. Until he has made it, a support email can sit unseen in Spam for thirty days and then be deleted.

**`[DECISION]` The support address is at the website's domain, and forwards to his own**
He first chose his Gmail address, then asked: "can we hide my email address?". An address on a public page cannot be hidden from the programs that collect them, with or without a script, and the app would show it in every draft. So the address is `support@milotriplog.top`. The domain's public records showed that it is at Namecheap and that its mail already went to Namecheap's free forwarding service (the MX records and the SPF line were there); he added one forwarder, `support` to his mailbox. **What it does not hide:** his replies come from his own address, to the people he answers. Sending as the support address would need a mail server that sends, which the forwarding does not include.

**`[CHANGE]` Version 0.3.1, build 4, is open**
0.3.0 is released, so the first change to the app after it raises the version (README, "The list of changes"): `versionName` 0.3.1, `versionCode` 4, and a 0.3.1 block in the list of changes without a date, which the What's new page shows as "Not released yet". The website part of this change is live with the merge; the tile reaches phones with 0.3.1's release.

### 2026-10-09

**`[FINDING]` 0.3.0 was published without the mascot's last fix, and published again with it**
Shawn asked for the release that evening: "i want to create a new release include in both github and upload to play store via production release". The mascot's work was still running in the background. Its checkout was clean and held one commit, and that was taken for finished: it was rebased, checked, merged (#41) and built, and 0.3.0 went out as a GitHub Release at 21:38 with that APK, which was also installed on the phone. Twenty minutes later the work's own report arrived. Its check had found the walking on the spot and the pale dial, and its fix (the entry below) was a second commit made after the first was taken. **The lesson: a background job is finished when it reports, not when its folder looks quiet,** and its work is not merged or released before that. What was done about it: the Play upload had not happened yet, so build number 3 was still free at Google; the published file had been downloaded by nobody (GitHub's own count, 0). Asked, Shawn chose to replace 0.3.0 over a 0.3.1: the fix was merged, the release's APK was replaced by one built from the new `main`, the tag `v0.3.0` was moved to it, and the bundle he uploads to Google Play is that build. **One build number, two binaries, for about an hour on one phone and on GitHub:** Android installs the second over the first without complaint, and nothing else ever held the first.

**`[CHANGE]` Version 0.3.0 is released**
On GitHub Releases as `MilO-0.3.0.apk` (39.9 MB; 64 MB with the 3D engine), signed with `~/keys/milo.jks`, and as a bundle for Google Play (15.5 MB; 25 MB), which Shawn uploads to the production track himself. It holds the seven lines of the list of changes: MilO's own clock, the mascot's walk, the Android Auto screen on one screen, the dollars on Home's month tile, a GPS fix every 2 seconds, the odometer that counts up, and the lower truck tile. 0.2.0 never had a GitHub Release: it went to Google Play's open testing only. The website's Play Store link waits, as a draft pull request (#42), until MilO's page there is public.

**`[FIX]` The mascot walked on the spot before he turned, and the big dial flickered: found by the check**

**`[CHANGE]` The mascot stands on the website's front page, and waves when a pointer is rolled over him**
Website only; nothing in the app changes. Shawn: "i would like to implment the mascot on the website to the right side of the biusiness mileage text block where he is just standing there. when you rollover the guy he will than wave". New: `website/mascot/standing.webp` (31 KB, every visitor gets it) and `wave.webp` (498 KB, fetched when a pointer first comes over the lime tile), the mascot in `index.html`, his rules in `styles.css`, and `.webp` in `firebase.json`'s week of caching. APP_ENCYCLOPEDIA, Website, step 7.
- **Where he stands, decided for Shawn:** from 900 pixels of width, 280 pixels high at the right of the paragraph and the button, under the headline; from 720, the same place at half the size; on a phone, half size under the words. **On a phone he is shown, not left out,** because most visitors to an Android app's page are on a phone and would never see him otherwise. It costs 152 pixels of tile there.
- **The headline is as it was:** one line at 1440 pixels, 52 pixels high. The tile there is 411 pixels high where it was 355: the paragraph is narrower beside him and runs to six lines from four. He adds no height himself.
- **He stands out on the lime.** His green is darker and bluer than the tile's, and his arms, legs and dials are black. The white gloves are the weakest part, light on light, and still read by their shading.
- **Checked** in headless Chrome 154 at eight widths from 360 to 1440: nothing wider than the screen, the other pages laid out as before. The rollover with a simulated pointer: the strip is not fetched with the page; it is fetched when the pointer comes over the tile; the wave runs once and starts again from its first frame at each rollover; nothing with "less motion" set; nothing on a simulated touch screen. **Not checked:** a hand on a mouse, Safari, Firefox, a real phone. Not deployed: that happens when this reaches `main`.

**`[DECISION]` The website stays without a script, so the stylesheet makes him wave**
The task had been written with a script in mind. The site has none on purpose, in three places: ADR-003 ("no JavaScript at all"), the Content-Security-Policy in `firebase.json` (`default-src 'none'`, which forbids a script file as much as an inline one) and the privacy page ("runs no scripts"). Shawn was not asked; the promise was kept. **What follows from it:**
- **The wave is not an animated picture.** A page without a script has no way to start one again from its first frame; whether a browser does so by itself when the picture is shown again was not tried and not relied on. So `wave.webp` is the wave's frames one under the other, and the stylesheet steps through them, which starts again every time.
- **Twelve frames a second, not the clip's 24.** The 24 frames are 498 KB at twice the size he is shown; 48 would be about a megabyte, as an animated picture too.
- **The frame's height is a multiple of 8 pixels** (`render_clips.py`), so that half of it is whole pixels on a Windows laptop scaled to 125 or 150 per cent. Otherwise he would shiver by part of a pixel from frame to frame.

**`[FINDING]` A stylesheet cannot know that a picture has arrived**
The first version took the standing picture away at the rollover and showed the strip. On a slow line (150 KB a second, the pointer brought straight onto him) the mascot was gone for two and a half seconds and came back standing, the wave missed. **Now the standing picture is never taken away,** and each frame of the strip covers it: where the standing mascot is and the waving one is not, the frame carries the tile's lime. On the same slow line he now stands through the first rollover and waves at the second.
- **The price: `wave.webp` carries `--accent`.** `pack_clips.py` reads it from `styles.css`. Change the colour and the strip has to be packed again, or a shape in the old lime waves with him. Said in `styles.css` at the colour and at the mascot, and in the mascot's README.
- WebP cannot store this lime exactly. The strip's is (199, 244, 51) where the tile's is (198, 244, 50): one step of 255 in red and in blue, measured in a screenshot, and not to be seen.

**`[DEBT]` No wave by a tap, and none that plays to its end once the pointer has left**
Both were asked for in the task and neither can be done without a script: a stylesheet has no rule for "he was tapped", and a rule that applies "while the pointer is over him" ends when it leaves. So a touch screen gets the standing mascot only, and a pointer that leaves in the middle of the wave finds him standing again at once. **Cost to fix:** a script of about 25 lines in a file of its own, a decision record beside ADR-003, `script-src 'self'` in the Content-Security-Policy, and "runs no scripts" on the privacy page turned into what the one script does. An hour, and Shawn's decision, because it changes a promise the site makes.

**`[CHANGE]` Three more clips for the mascot: flexing, the peace sign, the horns**
Shawn: "can we also create some more animations. a animation where he does some flexing poses 4-5 seoncds and one where he holds up the peace sign. and one last where he hold up devil finger. im not sure where i will use these but i would like to have them for future content ideas or use on the website". New in `animate_milo.py`: `Flex` (4.5 s), `Peace` (2.75 s) and `RockOn` (2.75 s), each from the standing pose and back to it. Drawn 400 pixels high on a see-through background into `design/mascot/renders/`: `milo_flex.webp` (1.48 MB), `milo_peace.webp` (0.96 MB), `milo_rock_on.webp` (0.95 MB), each played once, and a PNG still of each held pose. `render_clips.py` and `pack_clips.py` draw any clip this way. Nothing plays them yet. The Blender file and the `.glb` (2.21 MB, ten clips) were rebuilt from the scripts.
- **Decided for him:** `Flex` is both fists up beside the head, then the right arm up and the left down, the other way round, and both again, with a squeeze in each and the RPM needle jumping with it. `Peace` is the left hand, `RockOn` the right, the hand `Wave` and `ThumbsUp` use. He has three fingers: the peace sign is the two next to the thumb, the horns the two outer ones. `RockOn` nods three times to a beat.
- **The skeleton is not changed.** The signs need fingers that spread, and the finger bones could always turn that way; a pose now says by how much (`spread` in `arm_pose`), and the old poses say nothing.
- **The old clips are not changed:** built from the scripts as they were and from the new ones, the seven old clips come out the same in every key, bit for bit. The app's two pictures were not drawn again.
- **The dials are dulled in these pictures too,** by the greeting's own `dull_the_dials`, so he looks the same everywhere.

**`[FINDING]` What the mascot's build allows a clip**
- **The hands are large and the arms short.** A fist curled in towards the head, as a real arm flexes, is inside the head. `Flex` holds the fists beside its top corners, forearms leaning a little outwards, fingers to the viewer.
- **A hand beside the head must not swing past its place.** `ThumbsUp` does, for life; `Peace` and `RockOn` went through a dial when they did, and swing outwards as they arrive instead.
- **From "forearm up" to "forearm down" the shortest way can be through the body.** `curved()` draws the arm's way towards a third pose, round the outside.
- **Turned ten degrees to his right and leaning back, the dials go pale:** the same shine of their black faces that the greeting's turn showed (the `[FIX]` below). The pictures are drawn with it dulled, but the model keeps it, so `Flex` does not lean back in that pose.

**`[FINDING]` The Blender file is rebuilt without its window, and the rebuild repeats; the drawing does not**
The README's command opened Blender's window and left the saving to a hand. With `-b` and one more part that saves, it runs in three seconds without one (`design/mascot/README.md`).
- **Two rebuilds give the same clips and the same `.glb`, byte for byte.** That is how "the old clips are not changed" was checked.
- **The file committed until now was not quite its scripts' result:** about 850 of its 29,600 key values were off by less than a millionth, as from a file built in several sittings. Nothing to see. The file is now exactly what the scripts write.
- **Drawing is not repeatable to the byte.** The same file drawn twice on this Mac differs in every frame: a few dozen pixels, by 3 of 255 at most. So the app's pictures cannot be checked by drawing them again and comparing files. With this change the greeting's frames were compared instead, as the eye sees them: 14 pixels in 72 frames differ by more than 3 of 255, the largest by 5.

**`[FINDING]` The lime tile's own padding has never applied from 720 pixels of width**
In `styles.css`, `.hero { padding: 44px 40px; }` is followed in the same block by `.tile { padding: 28px 32px; }`, which is as strong and comes later, so the lime tile has had the other tiles' padding there all along. Left as it is: the page looks as Shawn has seen it, and the mascot is placed by the padding that applies. To be decided if the tile should ever have the wider one.

**`[FINDING]` One line too long brings the car's scrolling back, so the odometer is not on the car**
The first build of the two-row screen ended its Business line with "· Odometer 102,684 km", as the drawing Shawn chose had it. In the head unit, with his real figures, the line was 65 characters; about 50 fit at 800 by 480. It wrapped, the row grew by a line, and the arrows were back although everything was in view. A row's text cannot be measured from the app, and the figures grow through a month ("Today 199.9 km · September 2,202.5 km, $1,542" is 46 characters), so the line is kept to today, the month and its dollars, and the odometer, which the truck's dashboard shows anyway, was taken out again. This departs from "all the stuff on the home screen less todays trips" for one item; he was told when it was done.

**`[FINDING]` The Android Auto screen ran for the first time, in the Desktop Head Unit**
Shawn: "can we load android auto emulator to see how the app looks and acts?". The head unit (version 2.0, 7.4 MB from Google's SDK repository, checksum as in Google's list; asked first) is in `~/Library/Android/sdk/extras/google/auto/`. It needs a phone with Android Auto behind it, and an emulator has none that works without a Google sign-in, so his phone played that part: developer mode in Android Auto, "Unknown sources", "Start head unit server", then `adb forward tcp:5277 tcp:5277` and the head unit on the Mac. What it showed: the release build installed from the Mac **is listed and opens** in the head unit (the research said so for debug builds); the first connection was ended by the phone after a few seconds ("ByeBye"), the second held; the screen came back by itself after MilO was updated under it; and with the head unit connected MilO, waiting beside the parked truck, started nothing. The button was not pressed: it is his real log. Whether the truck lists a copy that is not from Google Play is still open (checks 89 and 90).

**`[FINDING]` Three rows do not fit the smallest car display**
At 800 by 480, the head unit's own size, the pane showed Status and This trip, the button, and scroll arrows for Today. Each row is a title and a line, about a fifth of the display's height. Two rows and the button fit. Shawn: "it needs to be on one screen no scrollable".

**`[CHANGE]` The Android Auto screen: two rows, Home's figures, the app's lime**
His three asks, looking at it: one screen, "all the stuff on the home screen less todays trips", and "make it similiar to the widget". An Android Auto app cannot draw its own layout, so he was shown what the library's templates allow as two drawings, a grid of tiles and "two lines and a big button", and chose the second. The rows are "This trip" (the status, and the trip's figures while one is open) and "Business" ("Today 17.9 km · October 412.3 km, $289"), each with a lime icon, and the button asks for the lime too. The titles stay fixed, so a redraw is still a refresh. **Today now means Business only,** as on Home; it was every finished trip. Nothing is counted by a rule of the car's own: `todayTrips`, `categoryTotals` and `allowanceCents` are Home's and the widget's. Android Auto only. Seen in the head unit at 21:19 with his real figures: one screen, no arrows, lime kept.

**`[CHANGE]` Home's month tile shows the month's Business dollars**
Shawn: "where it shows the October with the kilometers. Can we have on that the business dollar value that the person's getting back?". The tile's last line is "89% business · $289": the month's Business kilometres at the rate set in Settings, the widget's figure for the month, from the same function. Phone only (the car's Business row has it too, above). The tile is no taller: the dollars share the line the share already had. Seen on an emulator.

**`[DEBT]` The rate is still filed under the widget**
Home and the car price the month at the rate that was the widget's alone: stored as `home_widget_cents_per_km`, set on the widget's tile in Settings. Until now that tile showed the rate only while the widget was switched on, which would have left Home's dollars at a rate nobody could change; the tile now always shows it, with a line saying who else uses it. Cost to fix later: a tile of its own for the rate and a new stored name with a migration of the old one. Tagged in `HomeWidgetTile.kt`.

**`[CHANGE]` A trip is recorded with a GPS fix every 2 seconds, not every 5**
Shawn, 2026-10-09: "i think we should make the way point every 1 to 2 seconds and see if this fixes it before going further". `FixRate.RECORDING` is 2,000 ms. Nothing else in the trip engine moved: the rules count time and metres, never fixes, and the test tracks keep their 5-second spacing. The ten minutes of closer watching beside a parked truck used the trip's rate by name; they have a rate of their own now (`FixRate.WATCHING_CLOSELY`, 5 s), so that his choice of 2026-10-07 and what was proven about a truck driving off stay as they were. On an emulator a hand-started trip of 2,520 m stored 94 fixes, 89 of them 2.0 s apart, and measured 2,458 m. Both surfaces. ADR-002, amendment 37; device checks R2-1 to R2-4.

**`[FINDING]` The export does not show where 10 km went**
He compared a drive from work to his parents' with his truck's reading: MilO about 10 km short. In his export of that evening (33 trips, 9,521 points): the stored distance of his ordinary trips is within about 1 % of the sum of the phone's own speed readings; thinned to every second fix they lose 0.54 % (0.2 to 0.5 % on highway, up to about 3 % on short town trips); the evening's trips join end to start; the drive itself is two trips, 19.3 km saved as Business and 20.1 km saved as Personal, because he stopped for 17 minutes on the way. So a faster rate should give back a fraction of a kilometre on such a drive. He was told so and chose to try it first. If the gap stays, the first thing to settle is which two figures were compared (the drive is two trips in MilO, and only one of them is Business), then rule 3's threshold and the truck's own count.

**`[FINDING]` A faster rate gains on bends taken at speed and almost nothing on slow turns**
Rule 3 counts a step only once the truck is twice the fixes' stated error from where it last counted: 16 m with the 4 m fixes his phone gives (median 3.8 m while moving, 87 % at 5 m or better). At 25 km/h a fix every 2 s is 14 m on, so a step is counted every 28 m, against 35 m at 5 s: the corner is cut much as before (1.8 % against 2.2 % on the simulated town route). At 50 km/h it is 28 m against 70 m (1.7 % against 4.2 %). With poor fixes the threshold decides and the rate hardly matters. A first version of the new test asserted "2 s is never worse than 5 s" at 5 m accuracy and failed: on that route a 12 m by 16 m corner is exactly the 20 m threshold, and which side of it a fix falls on is rounding. The test uses 4 m, where nothing sits on the line.

**`[FINDING]` An overloaded emulator restarts Android in the middle of a trip, and MilO picks the trip up**
During the first emulator drive Android's own services restarted (two emulators and a build were running; load 15). Twenty seconds of fixes, a gap of 137 s, then "Process started", "picked up the stored state", the trip service back in the foreground and fixes every 2.0 s again, on the same trip. Nothing in MilO failed; the second drive, on a quiet Mac, had no gap. Worth knowing before reading a gap in an emulator run as a fault in MilO.

**`[DEBT]` Raw points grow two and a half times as fast**
By his first week of driving, about 83 MB a year in `points.db` (33 MB at 5 s) and about 104 MB a year in an export (42 MB). `points.db` is outside the cloud backup, so that limit is not touched; but an export passes the 25 MB most mail services take as an attachment after about three months instead of seven, and nothing thins or prunes old points. Cost to fix later: a rule for thinning the points of old trips (the distance of a closed trip is stored and would have to stay as recorded), or going back to 5 s if R2-2 shows no gain. Not tagged in code: there is no line of code that is wrong, only a number that grows.

**`[DECISION]` The 3D engine goes out again: the mascot is played from pictures, and walks in (ADR-007)**
The evening of the day Filament went in, the 0.3.0 build was measured: about 6 MB more for every phone from Google Play (`libfilament-jni.so` 2.8 MB and `libgltfio-jni.so` 3.0 MB), the website's APK 64 MB where it had been 39 MB, the bundle for Google Play 25 MB where it had been 15 MB. Shawn: "i wonder is there a better way to add the animations to the app without a bloated bundle. it seems frivilous and bloat ware. maybe a transparent animation?" Shown the cost and four ways on (switch to a transparent animated picture before 0.3.0; ship 0.3.0 as it was and switch after; keep 3D and trim it; remove the greeting), he chose "Switch before 0.3.0". A few minutes later he asked for a new greeting: "can we still modify the wave animation. i was thinking it would be cool if the mascot walked from the right side of the screen to the center at the bottom of the screen. walking just above the bottom navigation than turned and waved". ADR-007 holds both and supersedes ADR-005. **Decided with it and not asked of him, each a number in one place:** the mascot is 150 dp high; he walks turned 55 degrees from the viewer; he takes his steps twice as fast as the Walk clip; he turns with a small hop; the greeting takes no press from the screen and does not dim it, so "tap to skip" is gone.

**`[CHANGE]` The greeting is a walk, a turn and a wave, from two pictures; Filament is out**
Phone only. **Built, not yet proven on the phone.** New: the two pictures in `app/src/main/res/drawable-nodpi/` (98 KB and 782 KB), `MascotWalk.kt` (where the mascot is at each moment, pure), `render_greeting.py` and `pack_greeting.py` in `design/mascot/scripts/`. Rewritten: `Mascot.kt`, `MascotGreeting.kt`, `GreetingViewModel`. Removed: Filament and gltfio from the build, `MascotStage.kt`, the app's copy of the model (`assets/mascot/milo_wave.glb`) and `MascotModelTest`, the safety catch with `GreetingStorage.kt` and its count in the settings, the rule's fifth condition ("not on a phone the drawing has failed on"), and the two strings a screen reader was told. APP_ENCYCLOPEDIA, The greeting; device checks MG-1 to MG-9, rewritten under their numbers.
- **The sizes, on release builds of 0.3.0:** the website's APK is 39.9 MB (64 MB with Filament), the bundle for Google Play 15.5 MB (25 MB). The native libraries left are AndroidX's, as before the mascot. The mascot now costs 0.88 MB.
- **The safety catch is gone because its reason is.** It guarded against native code that ends the app with nothing to catch. A picture that cannot be read is an error the app catches: no greeting for that start, and one `ERROR` line in the Log. No phone ever ran the 3D build, so no phone holds the old count; a settings file that did would simply keep a value nobody reads.
- **What is not obvious from the code:** the app plays the walk and Android plays the turn and wave (the `[FIX]` above says why). The walk is a whole number of cycles because the turn begins with the frame that follows the walk's last one. A press that leaves Home ends the greeting at once, through the rule, not through the greeting.
- **The What's new line was corrected, not added to:** nobody has had the 3D version. "Tap anywhere to skip him" is no longer true.
- **Run on an emulator only** (Android 16, drawn without a graphics chip). Seen: the walk in from the right, the turn, the wave, the fade; Home not dimmed; clean edges on the dark page and on the lime and amber tiles (with the emulator on its side); Start trip pressed while he walked, and the trip started; nothing with animations off; put away in the middle and brought back, no mascot and no second greeting; the same after a turn of the phone.
- **What did not look right in the first build:** the mascot walked on the spot in the middle for 0.10 to 0.54 seconds before he turned, and a planted foot moved up to 6 dp within a step. Both are fixed (the `[FIX]` above). **What is left:** the walk takes 4 seconds on a phone, where the plan had said about 2 (the `[FINDING]` below).

**`[FINDING]` The Walk clip's stride sets how long the walk takes**
The clip walks on the spot, and each planted foot goes back 0.30 of a unit on a mascot 2 units high. Drawn 150 dp high and turned 55 degrees, that is 36.9 dp on the screen for one cycle of two steps (measured where the camera sees the soles: 47.7 pixels for the nearer foot and 44.5 for the farther, at 2.5 pixels to the dp; the nearer foot checked in the drawn frames, 16.0 pixels over two frames against 15.9). From beyond the right edge to the middle of a phone 393 dp wide is 263 dp: 8 cycles. At the clip's own speed that is 8 seconds, so every second frame is drawn and it is 4. The plan had said "about 2 seconds"; that would be nine steps a second, or feet that slide. **A shorter walk needs a longer stride in `animate_milo.py`,** which is the mascot's design.
- **Turned 68 degrees, as first tried, the face is lost:** the body is a slab 1.06 wide and 0.44 deep, and the M and the dials are then seen from the side. At 55 they read.
- **The studio's camera looks down on the ground.** So the foot nearer the viewer stands about 11 dp lower in the picture than the other, and a planted foot rises about 3 dp while it steps back: the mascot walks a little toward the viewer, and the app moves him straight to the left. A camera at the height of the ground would show neither, and would not be the look of `renders/milo_thumbs_up.png`.

**`[FINDING]` Three things about Android's animated pictures that cost a run each**
- **A stopped picture must keep its listeners.** `stop()` tells them a moment later, from a list it reads only then; `clearAnimationCallbacks()` in between ended the app with a NullPointerException inside `AnimatedImageDrawable.postOnAnimationEnd`. Found by pressing the Trips link under the mascot in the middle of his walk. The greeting only stops its pictures now.
- **"Ended" comes when the last frame is first drawn,** not when its time is up: 2,487 ms into a picture of 2,520.
- **Android keeps the picture's time and never leaves a frame out.** When the screen is drawn too slowly, the picture falls behind the clock and does not catch up. And it moves on only while some part of it is on the screen. Both are why the walk is no longer an animated picture (the `[FIX]` above).

**`[FINDING]` Where the pictures' bytes go**
At 3 pixels to the dp and quality 80, `img2webp` made 1.4 MB of the two. Two fifths of every frame was the see-through channel, which `img2webp` cannot be told to store more coarsely. So each frame is packed by `cwebp` (colour 60, see-through channel 50) and the frames are joined by `webpmux`. Fewer pixels saved little: the edges and the dials cost the bytes, not the flat green. At 2.5 pixels to the dp the two were 873 KB and looked like the frames on a phone (880 KB since the `[FIX]` above); at 2 they would be 712 KB and softer. The aim was 700 KB, the limit 1 MB. No dark or light fringe shows on the dark page or on the lime tile: without `-exact`, `cwebp` gives the see-through pixels beside an edge that edge's colour.

**`[DEBT]` The walk trusts the picture to keep time**
The app cannot ask an animated picture which frame it shows. The mascot is moved by the clock; if the phone draws too slowly the picture falls behind, and the mascot marks time in the middle until the picture's last step (0.10 to 0.54 seconds in the check's five recordings on the emulator, which draws without a graphics chip; never run on a phone). Between two frames of the picture the planted foot also moves about 3 dp with the mascot and is put back by the next frame. **Cost to fix:** carry the walk as one picture with its twelve frames side by side and let the app draw the frame its own clock asks for, so that the step and the place come from one clock.
**Closed 2026-10-09 (the same evening), before the merge:** done as written here (the `[FIX]` at the top of this date). The tag is gone from `MascotGreeting.kt`.

**`[CHANGE]` Version 0.3.0, build 3**
Shawn uploaded a bundle of 0.2.0 (build 2) to Google Play's open testing on the morning of 2026-10-09. The odometer that counts up, the mascot's greeting, the lower truck tile and MilO's own clock were merged later that day. Google Play takes a build number once, so a bundle with them needs a new one. Offered build 3 of 0.2.0, he chose a new version: "i think we will make a new version today build 3 verio 0.3.0 for all changes". **What was done:** `versionName` is 0.3.0 and `versionCode` 3. In the list of changes, 0.2.0 keeps the five changes its bundle carried and is dated 2026-10-09, the day that bundle went to Google Play; 0.3.0 holds the four merged since and is dated the same day, because it is being released that day (README, "Making a release", step 2, done in this pull request and not in one of its own). The split is by what each build contains, read from `git log` against the bundle's commit (dfe5b1b). **Not done here:** building, installing and publishing 0.3.0 (README, steps 3 to 5, and the bundle for Google Play). After the update the phone opens What's new once, as for any new version.

**`[FIX]` Two decision records were both numbered ADR-005**
The mascot's renderer was merged as ADR-005 at 16:15 on 2026-10-09 (#35). MilO's clock was merged as ADR-005 at 18:26 (#36): its branch had taken the number that morning, when 004 was the newest, and the last rebase did not look again. Found an hour later while reading the build file for the change above. The clock's record is now `docs/adr/ADR-006-trusted-clock.md`, and the 70 places that named it say ADR-006 (code comments, tests and documents; no code changed). ADR-005 is the mascot's renderer and nothing else. **For the next record:** look at `docs/adr/` on `main` at the moment of the merge, not when the branch is begun.

**`[DECISION]` The mascot is a 3D model, and the app draws it with Filament (ADR-005)**
Shawn built a mascot in Blender with Claude this day (a green block, two gauges for eyes, the app's "M", hose limbs, gloves, shoes; seven clips; `design/mascot/`), and asked: "please add the dependency and also upload both the image and model to the repo to use later. For now for the app i am only sure on using one of the animations. the wave one when the app starts up". Four questions were put to him first, as CLAUDE.md asks, each with a recommendation; he took all four: "Filament 3D" (over SceneView, and over a picture rendered beforehand with no library at all, which is where STANDARDS §2 points while the wave is the only use); "Greeting over Home"; "Phone only"; "Model, image and source" in the repository, which is public. STANDARDS §2 lets a library in only with code that needs it, so the dependency and the greeting are one change. Filament 1.77.3 was the newest on Maven Central that day; `filament-utils` was left out as not needed.

**`[CHANGE]` MilO greets: on a fresh start the mascot waves over Home**
Phone only. New: `feature/greeting/` (the rule and the safety catch), `Mascot`, `MascotGreeting` and `MascotStage` in the design system, `data/settings/GreetingStorage.kt`, `greetingAsked` in `MainActivity`, the model in `app/src/main/assets/mascot/`, and `design/mascot/` outside the app. APP_ENCYCLOPEDIA, The greeting; device checks MG-1 to MG-9.
- **What is not obvious from the code:** the greeting does not come later if its moment is taken (the first start, What's new, a tapped notification); it is cut, not faded, when What's new opens under it; and its clock starts when the mascot is seen, not when he was asked for.
- **Run on an emulator, not on the phone.** Android 16, the PC's graphics card: the wave over Home, nothing with animations off, the count cleared after a greeting.
- **What it costs:** the debug APK with all four processor types went from 46.6 MB to 70.1 MB. Shawn had been told "+13 MB on the website's APK" when he chose; that was the size of the libraries as Maven holds them, compressed, and an APK stores them uncompressed. He is told the true figure with this change.

**`[FINDING]` Native code that fails takes the app with it: the greeting has a safety catch**
On the emulator's software graphics (SwiftShader, `-gpu swiftshader_indirect`) the first greeting ended MilO: "Link error in base_lit_opaque ... Vertex shader active uniforms exceed GL_MAX_VERTEX_UNIFORM_VECTORS (256)". No exception: Filament stops the process. With the PC's graphics card (`-gpu host`) the same build drew. Phones' chips have higher limits, but none has run this yet, and a greeting at every start would turn one such phone into a MilO that cannot be opened. So each greeting is counted before the drawing and the count is cleared when the mascot is seen; two counted and MilO stops greeting there (`GreetingViewModel`). Two, because the count also stays when Android ends MilO in that moment for its own reasons.

**`[FINDING]` Three things about Filament that cost a run each**
- **The model's clips cannot be asked for before its parts are loaded.** `asset.instance.animator` before `ResourceLoader.loadResources` gives an animator that throws "Using Animator on destroyed asset" at its first use.
- **Filament's `UiHelper` does not ask a `TextureView` that already has its surface how large it is.** It reports the size it was told to wish for, 0 by 0 unless `setDesiredSize` was called, and the mascot is then drawn into nothing: no error, an empty view. `MascotStage` is built after the model is read, when the view has its surface, so it says the size first.
- **The first picture is late.** On the emulator about a second passed between asking and seeing, while the graphics chip compiled; with the clock running from the asking, half the wave was over before it was seen, and the screen had dimmed over nothing. Hence the clock from the third picture Filament takes, and no dimming before it.

**`[FINDING]` Blender's glTF exporter writes every clip that moves a bone, unless told not to**
`export_anim_single_armature`, on by default, adds every action in the file that moves a bone of the one skeleton, whether or not it is stashed on an NLA track and whether or not its strip is muted. The app's copy of the model was meant to hold the wave alone and held five clips (1.69 MB) until the option was switched off (1.16 MB). The two gauge clips had been left out all along: they key a slider, not a bone.

**`[DEBT]` The website's APK carries the 3D libraries for emulators too**
About 13 of the 23.5 MB the mascot added are Filament for `x86` and `x86_64`, which no phone uses. An `abiFilters` line for release builds would take them out; it is a build decision of its own (the emulator needs them in debug builds) and was not made here. Cost to fix: a few lines in `app/build.gradle.kts` and a check that the Play bundle and the website's APK still install.
**Closed 2026-10-09 (the same evening):** Filament is out of the build (ADR-007, the `[CHANGE]` at the top of this date), so the APK carries no 3D library for any processor, and the tag is gone from the code. What it still carries for `x86` and `x86_64` is AndroidX's own, 2.4 MB, as before the mascot.

**`[FIX]` MilO's clock ran on an emulator: what it found, and what was done**
The build with the two decisions below (commit `65851d8`) ran on a stock-Android emulator on 2026-10-09 (Android 16, API 36.1, `emulator-5680`, shut down afterwards; nothing was sent to the phone). MilO was installed fresh, with a stand-in vehicle, a parked limit of 5 minutes and the daily check at 06:00. The date was set a day ahead through the emulator's Settings 18 times, for 12 to 20 seconds each, and left changed twice. Each change was confirmed in Android's own time log. **It has still not run on the phone, and what this entry changed has run in unit tests only.**
- **No trip, wait, hold-off or stored GPS point was changed by any jump.**
  - **Normal use:** a trip started by button is stored at the phone's time of the press, and every GPS point is stamped with the phone's time to the millisecond.
  - **MilO idle:** one line, "The phone's date was set 24 h 0 min ahead. MilO saw it back 16 s later and kept its own time." The look at the jump said "no notification: 1 trip was started or is being recorded today, Fri 2026-10-09". Nothing was stored for tomorrow, and both alarms stood for the real next day.
  - **No MilO running:** the daily alarm started MilO inside the jump. Its lines are dated Friday, and the outcome was the same.
  - **Driving at speed:** one trip, 5,094 m of 5,100 fed, Business.
  - **The parked countdown:** the trip closed five minutes after the truck stopped, not at the jump.
  - **The first hour of a wait, and five jumps five minutes apart:** GPS stayed on at its rate, each jump left its line, and the next drive started by itself.
  - **The hold-off after End:** still held off, no trip, no sound.
  - **Start pressed inside the jump:** the phone read Saturday 16:42:08 and the trip is stored at Friday 16:42:08, "saved as Business: it started on a Fri".
  - **The alarms inside a jump** were read from Android: both counted from boot to the real 23:00 and 09:00, one delivery, no burst. One second after the return both were normal alarms again. With the date left ahead and MilO frozen, the counted alarm came at the real time and did not repeat.
- **Found, major: the first MilO process of a boot, started inside the jump, showed "No trip recorded today".** The emulator was restarted and the trick done before any MilO process had run. Android started MilO in the second the date went ahead. With no anchor of that boot MilO took Saturday. Three seconds later it showed the notification, on a channel that sounds, after a Friday with six trips. It stored the day as shown, asked both alarms for Sunday, and wrote 14 lines dated Saturday. When the date came back 12 seconds later, decision B worked as decided: the line "MilO started while…", the notification taken away, the stored day forgotten, both alarms asked for Saturday. This was the debt written down with B. **Fixed, by the decision below in this entry.**
- **Found, minor: a clock that is set BACK left both alarms waiting for a time of day.** A date set ahead makes Android deliver both alarms, and each then asks to count from boot. Set back, nothing is delivered, so nothing asked: the alarms stayed for 06:00 and 09:00 by a clock MilO did not believe, for the ten minutes until MilO followed. **Fixed:** when `ClockWatch` sets a backward change aside it has both `arm` functions ask once ("the phone's clock was set back and MilO keeps its own time"), and they count from boot by themselves. Only for a step back: for a step ahead it would add three lines to every trick for nothing. His trick goes ahead first and never came here.
- **Found, minor: "an anchor that was right is proven after two minutes" is true only for a MilO that stays awake.** Android froze a MilO that it had started and left alone within seconds, and the 30-second look is a wait inside that process. Two and a half minutes after it was taken, the anchor of such a MilO was still on probation; it was proven when MilO was opened. Nothing went wrong from it. The sentence is corrected below and in `ClockWatch.kt`, **and the decision in this entry wakes such a MilO two minutes on.**
- **Found, minor, not changed: a date left ahead is followed only by a MilO that watches.** Idle in the background, MilO was frozen 45 seconds after the change and had not followed after 10.5 minutes. Opened and left on the screen, it followed exactly ten minutes later. Nothing was lost: the alarms counted from boot. It is the debt "A change of the clock that is meant is followed late" of 2026-10-07, now seen. To follow without being opened, the look would have to be an alarm and not a wait inside the process. Not his habit; for Shawn to decide.
- **Found, minor, not changed: each trick writes seven lines with MilO alive,** and 13 to 17 when a process is started for it: three at the jump (the check asks to count, its look, the reminder asks to count), the clock's line, and three at the return. Five tricks in an evening gave 35 lines, five of them the clock's. They are how the fix is seen working on the phone, so they stay for now.
- **Found, minor, not changed: the header and Home keep the old day when MilO follows a held change with its screen open,** until MilO is brought to the front again. It is the same at midnight and older than the clock. His trick never reaches it.
- **Found, minor: the documents said the clock had run nowhere.** ADR-006, the encyclopedia and the device checks now say: on an emulator on 2026-10-09, not on the phone.
- **Not run on the emulator:** real Bluetooth, the sounds by ear, Android Auto, the widget, a wait of over 48 hours, the month's last evening, and the known gap of two jumps (CJ-13). HyperOS may freeze MilO, deliver `TIME_SET` and start MilO at boot differently.
- **Decided while fixing, beyond what was handed over: a clock on probation shows no notification, and is woken two minutes on.** For Shawn to confirm or have taken out.
  - **No notification.** While MilO's anchor is on probation, a "No trip recorded today" or a monthly reminder that would be due is not shown and not stored. The Log says "…no notification yet: no trip has been started today, Thu 2026-10-08, but MilO's time is not confirmed yet. It was taken from the phone's clock at a start with nothing to check it against, and the phone's date may be set ahead. MilO looks again once its time is confirmed." The rules that decide whether one is due are not changed (`judgeNothingRecorded`, `judgeReminder`).
  - **Woken two minutes on.** While its clock agrees with the phone's and is on probation, MilO asks for both daily alarms 2 minutes ahead (`PROBATION_MS`), counted from boot, and not for the next day. When that alarm comes, the reading confirms the clock, or sees the date back and starts two more minutes. Then the alarm is asked for in the normal way, and a notification that is truly due is shown. Without it, a notification held back in a MilO that Android freezes would wait for the next day's alarm. It also closes two holes that were there before: such a MilO kept the alarm of a wrong day until something else read the time, and its anchor stayed unconfirmed for as long.
  - **No loop.** If the phone's clock goes ahead of an anchor on probation and stays, MilO asks for the alarm that counts to the real time (decision A), not for two more minutes again and again.
  - **What it costs in normal use.** After every restart of the phone, and once after the update that brings this clock, the phone is woken once more, two minutes after MilO's first start, and the Log has a few more lines. A notification that is due at that start comes two to four minutes later, as the alarm is inexact. On a phone that gives no boot number (CJ-1) this would be so at every start of MilO; there it is also what keeps a start inside a jump quiet.
  - **In code.** `platform/clock/DailyAlarmAsked.kt`: `askForDailyAlarm` decides between the three ways for both looks, so the two cannot come to differ. `MonthlyReminder` and `NothingRecordedCheck` are handed one more function, `clockOnProbation`. `ReminderVerdict.said()` moved to `ReminderLogText.kt` to keep `MonthlyReminder.kt` under the 300 lines of STANDARDS §3.
- **Tests.** 14 are new: `NothingRecordedOnProbationTest` (4: a start inside the jump shows nothing and stores nothing; frozen and never told, it is woken two minutes on and never asks for the wrong day; after a restart with the date right a notification that is due comes two minutes on; a date that stays ahead during the probation gets the counted alarm), `MonthlyReminderOnProbationTest` (2), `DailyAlarmAskedTest` (6), and one each in `ClockWatchTest` and `NothingRecordedAlarmInAJumpTest` for the clock set back.
